[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Iterate through the given list of numbers.
2. For each number, iterate through the list again and calculate the absolute difference between the current number and all other numbers.
3. If the absolute difference is less than the given threshold, return True.
4. If no such pair of numbers is found, return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def has_close_elements(numbers: List[float], threshold: float) -> bool:
    for i in range(len(numbers)):
        for j in range(i+1, len(numbers)):
            if abs(numbers[i] - numbers[j]) < threshold:
                return True
    return False
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to separate the groups of balanced parentheses in the input string.
2. We can achieve this by iterating over the characters in the string and keeping track of the opening and closing parentheses.
3. Whenever we encounter an opening parenthesis '(', we start a new group and capture characters until we encounter a closing parenthesis ')'.
4. We can ignore any spaces in the input string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def separate_paren_groups(paren_string: str) -> List[str]:
    result = []
    temp_group = []
    in_group = False
    
    for char in paren_string:
        if char == '(':
            in_group = True
            temp_group.append(char)
        elif char == ')' and in_group:
            temp_group.append(char)
            result.append(''.join(temp_group))
            temp_group = []
            in_group = False
        elif in_group:
            temp_group.append(char)
    
    return result
```

from typing import List

def separate_paren_groups(paren_string: str) -> List[str]:
    result = []
    temp_group = []
    in_group = False
    
    for char in paren_string:
        if char == '(':
            in_group = True
            temp_group.append(char)
        elif char == ')' and in_group:
            temp_group.append(char)
            result.append(''.join(temp_group))
            temp_group = []

            temp_group.append(char)
    
    if temp_group:  # Handling the case where the last group might not have been fully processed
        result.append(''.join(temp_group))
    
    return result

2. We can achieve this by converting the floating-point number to an integer, which will automatically truncate the decimal part. Then, subtract this integer from the original floating-point number to get the decimal part.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def truncate_number(number: float) -> float:
    integer_part = int(number)
    decimal_part = number - integer_part
    return decimal_part
```

4. If the `balance` falls below zero at any point, return True. Otherwise, return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def below_zero(operations: List[int]) -> bool:
    balance = 0
    for operation in operations:
        balance += operation
        if balance < 0:
            return True
    return False
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To calculate the Mean Absolute Deviation (MAD) for a given list of numbers, we first need to calculate the mean of the numbers.
2. Then, for each number in the list, we calculate the absolute difference between the number and the mean.
3. We take the average of all these absolute differences to get the Mean Absolute Deviation.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def mean_absolute_deviation(numbers: List[float]) -> float:
    mean = sum(numbers) / len(numbers)  # Calculate the mean of the numbers
    absolute_diff_sum = sum(abs(x - mean) for x in numbers)  # Calculate the sum of absolute differences
    mad = absolute_diff_sum / len(numbers)  # Calculate the Mean Absolute Deviation
    return mad
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Define a function named `intersperse` that takes two arguments - a list of integers `numbers` and an integer `delimeter`.
2. Initialize an empty list to store the result.
3. Iterate through the input `numbers` list.
4. For each element in the list, add the element and the `delimeter` to the result list.
5. Return the result list.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def intersperse(numbers: List[int], delimeter: int) -> List[int]:
    result = []
    for num in numbers:
        result.extend([num, delimeter])
    return result[:-1]  # Remove the last delimeter
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to parse the input string which contains multiple groups of nested parentheses separated by spaces.
2. For each group, we need to determine the deepest level of nesting of parentheses.
3. To achieve this, we can iterate over the groups, count the opening and closing parentheses to determine the level of nesting.
4. We will keep track of the maximum nesting level encountered for each group.
5. Finally, we will return a list containing the maximum nesting level for each group in the order they appear in the input string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def parse_nested_parens(paren_string: str) -> List[int]:
    def find_deepest_nesting(paren_group):
        max_nesting_level = 0
        current_nesting_level = 0
        for char in paren_group:
            if char == '(':
                current_nesting_level += 1
                max_nesting_level = max(max_nesting_level, current_nesting_level)
            elif char == ')':
                current_nesting_level -= 1
        return max_nesting_level
    
    nested_levels = []
    paren_groups = paren_string.split()
    for group in paren_groups:
        nested_levels.append(find_deepest_nesting(group))
    
    return nested_levels
```

```python
from typing import List, Tuple

def sum_product(numbers: List[int]) -> Tuple[int, int]:
    sum_result = 0
    product_result = 1
    for num in numbers:
        sum_result += num
        product_result *= num
    return sum_result, product_result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize an empty list to store the rolling maximum elements.
2. Iterate through the input list of numbers.
3. At each iteration, find the maximum element from the current element and all the previous elements.
4. Append this maximum element to the rolling maximum list.
5. Return the rolling maximum list.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def rolling_max(numbers: List[int]) -> List[int]:
    rolling_max_list = []
    for i in range(len(numbers)):
        rolling_max_list.append(max(numbers[:i+1]))
    return rolling_max_list
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To find the shortest palindrome that begins with the supplied string, we need to follow these steps:
    - Find the longest postfix of the supplied string that is a palindrome.
    - Append to the end of the string the reverse of the string prefix that comes before the palindromic suffix.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_palindrome(string: str) -> bool:
    """ Test if given string is a palindrome """
    return string == string[::-1]


def make_palindrome(string: str) -> str:
    """ Find the shortest palindrome that begins with a supplied string """
    if not string:
        return ''
    
    for i in range(len(string), 0, -1):
        if is_palindrome(string[:i]):
            return string + string[:i-1][::-1]
    
    return string + string[:-1][::-1]
```

def is_palindrome(string: str) -> bool:
    """ Test if given string is a palindrome """
    return string == string[::-1]

def make_palindrome(string: str) -> str:
    """ Find the shortest palindrome that begins with a supplied string """
    if not string:
        return ''

    for i in range(len(string), 0, -1):
        if is_palindrome(string[:i]):
            return string + string[i:][::-1]

    return string + string[:-1][::-1]
```

def make_palindrome(string: str) -> str:
    """ Find the shortest palindrome that begins with a supplied string """
    if not string:
        return ''

    for i in range(len(string), 0, -1):
        if is_palindrome(string[:i]):
            return string + string[i:][::-1]

    return string + string[:-1][::-1]
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We are given two binary strings `a` and `b`.
2. We need to perform XOR operation on these two strings.
3. XOR operation compares corresponding bits of two operands and returns 1 if they are different, and 0 if they are the same.
4. We will iterate through both strings and perform XOR operation on each pair of corresponding bits.
5. Finally, we will return the result as a binary string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def string_xor(a: str, b: str) -> str:
    result = ''
    for char_a, char_b in zip(a, b):
        if char_a != char_b:
            result += '1'
        else:
            result += '0'
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the input list is empty. If it is empty, return None.
2. Iterate over the list of strings and keep track of the longest string seen so far.
3. If we encounter a string that is longer than the current longest string, update the longest string.
4. If we encounter a string of the same length as the current longest string, keep the current longest string (as we need to return the first one in case of multiple strings of the same length).
5. Return the longest string found.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
from typing import List, Optional

def longest(strings: List[str]) -> Optional[str]:
    if not strings:
        return None

    longest_str = strings[0]
    for s in strings[1:]:
        if len(s) > len(longest_str):
            longest_str = s

    return longest_str
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to find the greatest common divisor (GCD) of two integers `a` and `b`.
2. We can use the Euclidean algorithm to find the GCD of two numbers.
3. The algorithm states that the GCD of two numbers `a` and `b` is the same as the GCD of `b` and `a % b`.
4. We apply this recursively until `b` becomes 0, at which point `a` will be the GCD.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def greatest_common_divisor(a: int, b: int) -> int:
    while b != 0:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a
```

2. We can define a dictionary to map the note symbols to the corresponding beat duration.
3. Then, we can iterate over the characters in the input string, check each character against the dictionary, and append the corresponding beat duration to the result list.
4. Finally, return the list of beat durations.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def parse_music(music_string: str) -> List[int]:
    note_duration = {'o': 4, 'o|': 2, '.|': 1}
    beats = []
    
    for note in music_string.split():
        beats.append(note_duration[note])
    
    return beats
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to find how many times a given substring can be found in the original string.
2. The function should count overlapping cases, meaning if the substring appears multiple times in the original string with overlaps, each occurrence should be counted.
3. We can achieve this by iterating over the original string and checking if the substring matches the corresponding slice of the original string.
4. For each match found, we increment a counter to keep track of the total number of occurrences.
5. Finally, we return the total count of occurrences of the substring in the original string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def how_many_times(string: str, substring: str) -> int:
    count = 0
    for i in range(len(string) - len(substring) + 1):
        if string[i:i + len(substring)] == substring:
            count += 1
    return count
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Create a dictionary to map each word numeral to its integer equivalent.
2. Split the input `numbers` string by spaces to get a list of word numerals.
3. Convert each word numeral to its corresponding integer using the dictionary from step 1.
4. Sort the list of integers in ascending order.
5. Map the sorted integers back to their word numeral representations.
6. Join the sorted word numerals with spaces to form the final sorted string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def sort_numbers(numbers: str) -> str:
    # Dictionary to map word numerals to integers
    numeral_mapping = {
        'zero': 0,
        'one': 1,
        'two': 2,
        'three': 3,
        'four': 4,
        'five': 5,
        'six': 6,
        'seven': 7,
        'eight': 8,
        'nine': 9
    }
    
    # Split the input string by spaces to get a list of word numerals
    numerals = numbers.split()
    
    # Convert word numerals to integers using the mapping
    numerals_int = [numeral_mapping[numeral] for numeral in numerals]
    
    # Sort the list of integers in ascending order
    sorted_numerals_int = sorted(numerals_int)
    
    # Map the sorted integers back to their word numeral representations
    sorted_numerals = [key for value, key in sorted(zip(sorted_numerals_int, numerals))]
    
    # Join the sorted word numerals with spaces to form the final sorted string
    sorted_numbers = ' '.join(sorted_numerals)
    
    return sorted_numbers
```

### Requirements

```python
from typing import List, Tuple

def find_closest_elements(numbers: List[float]) -> Tuple[float, float]:
    min_diff = float('inf')
    closest_pair = None
    
    for i in range(len(numbers)-1):
        for j in range(i+1, len(numbers)):
            diff = abs(numbers[i] - numbers[j])
            if diff < min_diff:
                min_diff = diff
                closest_pair = (min(numbers[i], numbers[j]), max(numbers[i], numbers[j]))
    
    return closest_pair
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Find the minimum and maximum values from the list of numbers.
2. Calculate the range of values by subtracting the minimum from the maximum.
3. For each number in the list, calculate the rescaled value using the formula: `(number - minimum) / range`.
4. Return the list of rescaled values.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def rescale_to_unit(numbers: List[float]) -> List[float]:
    min_num = min(numbers)
    max_num = max(numbers)
    range_num = max_num - min_num
    rescaled_numbers = [(num - min_num) / range_num for num in numbers]
    return rescaled_numbers
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to find the largest number that divides the given number `n` evenly, smaller than `n`.
2. We can start by initializing a variable `largest_div` to store the largest divisor found so far. We can start with `largest_div = 1`.
3. We will iterate from 1 to `n-1` and check if the current number divides `n` evenly. If it does, we will update `largest_div` if the current number is greater than the current `largest_div`.
4. After iterating through all numbers from 1 to `n-1`, we will have the largest divisor stored in `largest_div` and return it.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def largest_divisor(n: int) -> int:
    largest_div = 1
    for i in range(1, n):
        if n % i == 0:
            largest_div = max(largest_div, i)
    return largest_div
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize an empty list to store the prime factors.
2. Start dividing the input number `n` by 2 until it is divisible by 2. Add 2 to the list of prime factors each time it is divisible by 2.
3. After dividing by 2, start dividing by 3 until it is divisible by 3. Add 3 to the list of prime factors each time it is divisible by 3.
4. Continue this process for all odd integers starting from 3 up to the square root of `n`.
5. If the remaining `n` is greater than 2, add `n` to the list of prime factors.
6. Return the list of prime factors in sorted order.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def factorize(n: int) -> List[int]:
    factors = []
    divisor = 2
    
    while n > 1:
        if n % divisor == 0:
            factors.append(divisor)
            n //= divisor
        else:
            divisor += 1
    
    return factors
```

```python
from typing import List

def remove_duplicates(numbers: List[int]) -> List[int]:
    unique_numbers = set()
    result = []
    
    for num in numbers:
        if num not in unique_numbers:
            result.append(num)
            unique_numbers.add(num)
    
    return result

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Iterate through each character in the given string.
2. Check if the character is lowercase or uppercase.
3. If it is lowercase, convert it to uppercase and vice versa.
4. Return the modified string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def flip_case(string: str) -> str:
    flipped_string = ""
    for char in string:
        if char.islower():
            flipped_string += char.upper()
        elif char.isupper():
            flipped_string += char.lower()
        else:
            flipped_string += char
    return flipped_string
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the input number `n` is less than 2. If it is, return False as numbers less than 2 are not prime.
2. Iterate over numbers from 2 to the square root of `n` (rounded up to the nearest integer) inclusive.
3. For each number in the iteration, check if `n` is divisible by that number. If it is, return False as `n` is not prime.
4. If no number from 2 to the square root of `n` divides `n` evenly, return True as `n` is prime.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
import math

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, math.isqrt(n) + 1):
        if n % i == 0:
            return False
    return True
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. The `find_zero` function aims to find a zero point of a polynomial function defined by the coefficients provided in the input list `xs`.
2. To find the zero point, we need to solve the polynomial equation `poly(x) = 0`.
3. The `poly` function evaluates the polynomial using the coefficients in `xs` at a given point `x`.
4. Since the polynomial may have multiple roots, we need to return only one zero point. This is achieved by considering the largest non-zero coefficient in the polynomial.
5. We will iterate through a range of values for `x` to find the zero point where `poly(x) = 0`.
6. The `find_zero` function only accepts input lists `xs` with an even number of coefficients to ensure that there is a solution.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
import math

def poly(xs: list, x: float):
    return sum([coeff * math.pow(x, i) for i, coeff in enumerate(xs)])

def find_zero(xs: list):
    if len(xs) % 2 != 0:
        raise ValueError("Input list must have an even number of coefficients")
    
    max_coeff = max([abs(coeff) for coeff in xs])
    degree = len(xs) - 1
    epsilon = 1e-6  # small value for precision

    for i in range(-1000, 1001):  # Iterate through a range of values for x
        x = i / 1000
        if abs(poly(xs, x)) < epsilon and abs(poly(xs, x+epsilon)) > epsilon:
            return round(x, 6)
        
    raise ValueError("No zero point found within the range of -1 to 1")
```

1. The error occurred when the `find_zero` function could not find a zero point within the range of -1 to 1 for the given polynomial.
2. The function iterates through a range of values for `x` from -1 to 1 to find the zero point where `poly(x) = 0`.
3. If the zero point is not found within this range, a `ValueError` is raised.
4. The error indicates that no zero point was found within the specified range.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
import math
import copy

def poly(xs: list, x: float):
    return sum([coeff * math.pow(x, i) for i, coeff in enumerate(xs)])

def find_zero(xs: list):
    if len(xs) % 2 != 0:
        raise ValueError("Input list must have an even number of coefficients")
    
    max_coeff = max([abs(coeff) for coeff in xs])
    degree = len(xs) - 1
    epsilon = 1e-6  # small value for precision

    for i in range(-1000, 1001):  # Iterate through a range of values for x
        x = i / 1000
        if abs(poly(xs, x)) < epsilon and abs(poly(xs, x+epsilon)) > epsilon:
            return round(x, 6)
        
    raise ValueError("No zero point found within the range of -1 to 1")
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Create an empty list to store the final result.
2. Iterate over the indices of the input list.
3. Check if the index is divisible by 3.
    - If yes, extract the values at that index and the next two indices, sort them, and append to the final list.
    - If no, append the value at that index to the final list.
4. Return the final list as the output.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def sort_third(l: list):
    result = []
    for i in range(len(l)):
        if i % 3 == 0:
            sub_list = sorted(l[i:i+3])
            result.extend(sub_list)
        else:
            result.append(l[i])
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize a variable `count` to keep track of the number of times digit 7 appears.
2. Iterate over numbers from 1 to n-1.
3. Check if the number is divisible by either 11 or 13.
4. If the number is divisible by 11 or 13, convert the number to a string and count the occurrences of digit '7'.
5. Increment the `count` variable by the number of occurrences of digit '7'.
6. Return the final `count` value.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def fizz_buzz(n: int):
    count = 0
    for num in range(1, n):
        if num % 11 == 0 or num % 13 == 0:
            count += str(num).count('7')
    return count
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To decode the cyclic encoded string, we need to reverse the encoding process performed by the `encode_cyclic` function.
2. In the `encode_cyclic` function, the input string is split into groups of three characters and each group is cyclically shifted by one position to the left.
3. To decode the cyclic encoded string, we need to reverse this process by shifting each group of three characters by one position to the right.
4. We also need to handle cases where the last group may have fewer than 3 characters and adjust the decoding accordingly.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def decode_cyclic(s: str):
    # split string to groups. Each of length 3.
    groups = [s[(3 * i):min((3 * i + 3), len(s))] for i in range((len(s) + 2) // 3)]
    # cycle elements in each group to the right. Unless group has fewer elements than 3.
    groups = [(group[-1] + group[:-1]) if len(group) == 3 else group for group in groups]
    return "".join(groups)
```

def encode_cyclic(s: str):
    """
    returns encoded string by cycling groups of three characters.
    """
    # split string to groups. Each of length 3.
    groups = [s[(3 * i):min((3 * i + 3), len(s))] for i in range((len(s) + 2) // 3)]
    # cycle elements in each group. Unless group has fewer elements than 3.
    groups = [(group[1:] + group[0]) if len(group) == 3 else group for group in groups]
    return "".join(groups)

def decode_cyclic(s: str):
    """
    takes as input string encoded with encode_cyclic function. Returns decoded string.
    """
    # split string to groups. Each of length 3.
    groups = [s[(3 * i):min((3 * i + 3), len(s))] for i in range((len(s) + 2) // 3)]
    # cycle elements in each group to the right. Unless group has fewer elements than 3.
    groups = [(group[-1] + group[:-1]) if len(group) == 3 else group for group in groups]
    return "".join(groups)

```python
def is_prime(num):
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(num**0.5) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def prime_fib(n: int):
    def fibonacci(n):
        a, b = 0, 1
        for _ in range(n):
            yield a
            a, b = b, a + b
    
    count = 0
    for num in fibonacci(n**2):  # We generate Fibonacci numbers until the n-th prime Fibonacci number
        if is_prime(num):
            count += 1
            if count == n:
                return num

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to find three distinct elements in the input list that sum to zero.
2. To solve this problem, we will use a nested loop to iterate through all possible combinations of three elements from the input list.
3. For each combination, we will check if the sum of the three elements is zero.
4. If we find any such combination, we will return True, else we will return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def triples_sum_to_zero(l: list):
    if len(l) < 3:
        return False
    
    for i in range(len(l)-2):
        for j in range(i+1, len(l)-1):
            for k in range(j+1, len(l)):
                if l[i] + l[j] + l[k] == 0:
                    return True
    return False
```

from math import factorial

def car_race_collision(n: int):

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to iterate through the list of integers.
2. For each element in the list, we will check if there exists another element in the list that sums to zero with the current element.
3. To do this efficiently, we can use a set to keep track of the elements seen so far.
4. If the negative of the current element is present in the set, then we have found two distinct elements that sum to zero.
5. Return True if such a pair is found, otherwise return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def pairs_sum_to_zero(l):
    seen = set()
    for num in l:
        if -num in seen:
            return True
        seen.add(num)
    return False
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Define a function named `change_base` that takes two parameters `x` and `base`.
2. Convert the integer input number `x` to the desired base using the given `base`.
3. Return the string representation of the converted number.
4. The base numbers provided are less than 10, so we will be converting to bases less than 10.
5. For conversion, we need to repeatedly divide the number by the base and keep track of remainders until the number becomes 0.
6. The remainders will be the digits of the converted number in the new base.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def change_base(x: int, base: int):
    if x == 0:
        return '0'
    
    converted = ''
    while x > 0:
        remainder = x % base
        converted = str(remainder) + converted
        x //= base
    
    return converted
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to efficiently compute the n-th element of the fib4 number sequence without using recursion.
2. We can utilize a loop to calculate the values of fib4 sequence iteratively.
3. Initialize a list to store the values of the fib4 sequence up to n.
4. Based on the definition of the fib4 sequence, calculate each subsequent value by summing the last four values in the list.
5. Return the n-th element of the fib4 sequence.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def fib4(n: int):
    if n == 0:
        return 0
    elif n == 1:
        return 0
    elif n == 2:
        return 2
    elif n == 3:
        return 0
    
    fib_list = [0, 0, 2, 0]
    
    for i in range(4, n+1):
        next_val = fib_list[i-1] + fib_list[i-2] + fib_list[i-3] + fib_list[i-4]
        fib_list.append(next_val)
    
    return fib_list[n]
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Sort the input list `l` in ascending order.
2. Check if the length of the list is odd or even.
3. If the length is odd, return the middle element.
4. If the length is even, return the average of the two middle elements.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def median(l: list):
    l_sorted = sorted(l)
    n = len(l_sorted)
    if n % 2 == 1:
        return l_sorted[n // 2]
    else:
        return (l_sorted[n // 2 - 1] + l_sorted[n // 2]) / 2
```

2. We will iterate over each character in the encoded string and shift it back by 5 to get the decoded character.
3. To handle wrapping around the alphabet (e.g., shifting 'z' by 1 should give 'a'), we can use modulo operation and adjust the shift accordingly.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def decode_shift(s: str):
    return "".join([chr(((ord(ch) - 5 - ord("a")) % 26) + ord("a")) for ch in s])

def remove_vowels(text):
    vowels = 'aeiouAEIOU'
    result = ''
    for char in text:
        if char not in vowels:
            result += char
    return result

None

### Code
```

    opening_count = brackets.count('<')
    closing_count = brackets.count('>')
    
    return opening_count == closing_count

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. The function is designed to count the number of opening brackets `<` and closing brackets `>` separately and check if they are equal.
2. The error message indicates that the function is not working correctly for the input `"><<>"`. This is because it is only checking if the count of opening brackets is equal to the count of closing brackets, but it is not considering the correct order of brackets.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def correct_bracketing(brackets: str):
    stack = []
    
    for bracket in brackets:
        if bracket == '<':
            stack.append('<')
        elif bracket == '>':
            if not stack:
                return False
            else:
                stack.pop()
    
    return len(stack) == 0
```

1. We need to check if the elements in the list are monotonically increasing or decreasing.
2. To do this, we can iterate through the list and compare each element with the next one.
3. If all elements are either increasing or decreasing, then the list is monotonic.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def monotonic(l: list):
    increasing = all(l[i] <= l[i + 1] for i in range(len(l) - 1))
    decreasing = all(l[i] >= l[i + 1] for i in range(len(l) - 1))
    return increasing or decreasing
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to find the common elements between two lists.
2. First, we will convert the lists into sets to get unique elements from each list.
3. Then, we will find the intersection of the two sets to get the common elements.
4. Finally, we will return the sorted list of common elements.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def common(l1: list, l2: list):
    set1 = set(l1)
    set2 = set(l2)
    common_elements = sorted(list(set1.intersection(set2)))
    return common_elements
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Iterate through numbers starting from 2 (smallest prime number) up to the square root of n.
2. For each number, check if it is a factor of n.
3. If a number is a factor of n, check if it is a prime number.
4. Keep track of the largest prime factor found.
5. Return the largest prime factor.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
import math

def is_prime(num):
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(math.sqrt(num)) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def largest_prime_factor(n: int):
    largest_prime = 0
    for i in range(2, int(math.sqrt(n)) + 1):
        if n % i == 0:
            if is_prime(i):
                largest_prime = i
    return largest_prime

# Test cases
print(largest_prime_factor(13195))  # Output: 29
print(largest_prime_factor(2048))   # Output: 2
```

3. The function implementation identifies the largest prime factor of a given number correctly.
4. The `is_prime` function checks if a number is prime by iterating up to the square root of the number.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
import math

def is_prime(num):
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(math.sqrt(num)) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def largest_prime_factor(n: int):
    largest_prime = 0
    for i in range(2, int(math.sqrt(n)) + 1):
        if n % i == 0:
            if is_prime(i):
                largest_prime = i
    return largest_prime
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to check if every opening bracket has a corresponding closing bracket in the given string of brackets.
2. We can achieve this by iterating through each character in the string.
3. We will use a stack to keep track of opening brackets.
4. If we encounter an opening bracket, we push it onto the stack.
5. If we encounter a closing bracket, we check if the stack is empty (meaning no opening bracket for the closing bracket) or the top of the stack is not an opening bracket corresponding to this closing bracket.
6. If any of the above conditions are met, we return False.
7. If all brackets match correctly, we return True.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def correct_bracketing(brackets: str):
    stack = []
    opening_bracket = '('
    closing_bracket = ')'
    
    for bracket in brackets:
        if bracket == opening_bracket:
            stack.append(bracket)
        elif bracket == closing_bracket:
            if not stack or stack[-1] != opening_bracket:
                return False
            stack.pop()
    
    return len(stack) == 0
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. The derivative of a polynomial term ax^n is n * ax^(n-1).
2. Iterate through the list of coefficients, skipping the first element (constant term) as it does not affect the derivative.
3. For each coefficient at index i, multiply it by i to get the derivative coefficient.
4. Append the derivative coefficient to a new list.
5. Return the new list as the derivative of the polynomial.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def derivative(xs: list):
    derivative_coeffs = []
    for i in range(1, len(xs)):
        derivative_coeffs.append(xs[i] * i)
    return derivative_coeffs
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the input `n` is equal to 0, 1, or 2. If so, return the corresponding values (0, 0, 1) as per the definition of the FibFib sequence.
2. If `n` is greater than 2, use a loop to calculate the FibFib sequence up to the `n-th` element using the formula `fibfib(n) = fibfib(n-1) + fibfib(n-2) + fibfib(n-3)`.
3. Return the `n-th` element of the FibFib sequence.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def fibfib(n: int):
    if n == 0:
        return 0
    elif n == 1:
        return 0
    elif n == 2:
        return 1
    else:
        fibfib_seq = [0, 0, 1]
        for i in range(3, n+1):
            fib_n = fibfib_seq[i-1] + fibfib_seq[i-2] + fibfib_seq[i-3]
            fibfib_seq.append(fib_n)
        return fibfib_seq[n]

# Test the function with examples from the description
print(fibfib(1))  # Output: 0
print(fibfib(5))  # Output: 4
print(fibfib(8))  # Output: 24
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to count the number of vowels in a given word.
2. Vowels include 'a', 'e', 'i', 'o', 'u' and 'y' at the end of the word.
3. We will iterate through each character in the word and count the vowels.
4. If 'y' is at the end of the word, we will also count it as a vowel.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def vowels_count(s):
    vowels = ['a', 'e', 'i', 'o', 'u']
    count = 0
    for i in range(len(s)):
        if s[i].lower() in vowels:
            count += 1
        elif i == len(s) - 1 and s[i].lower() == 'y':
            count += 1
    return count
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Convert the integer x to a string to easily manipulate the digits.
2. Calculate the number of digits in x by using the `len()` function on the string representation of x.
3. Calculate the effective shift amount by taking the modulo of `shift` with the number of digits in x to handle cases where shift is greater than the number of digits.
4. Perform the circular shift by slicing the string from the correct index to the end and concatenating it with the sliced part from the beginning to the correct index.
5. Return the resulting string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def circular_shift(x, shift):
    x_str = str(x)
    num_digits = len(x_str)
    effective_shift = shift % num_digits
    
    if effective_shift == 0:
        return x_str
    
    shifted_str = x_str[-effective_shift:] + x_str[:-effective_shift]
    
    return shifted_str
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning

### Code
```python
def digitSum(s):
    total_sum = 0
    for char in s:
        if char.isupper():
            total_sum += ord(char)
    return total_sum

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning

3. Return the total number of mango fruits.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def fruit_distribution(s, n):
    # Split the string to extract the number of apples and oranges
    fruits = s.split()
    apples = int(fruits[0])
    oranges = int(fruits[3])
    
    # Calculate the total number of mango fruits
    mango = n - apples - oranges
    
    return mango
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize variables to keep track of the smallest even value and its index.
2. Iterate through the array and check if the current element is even and smaller than the current smallest even value.
3. If the condition is met, update the smallest even value and its index.
4. If there are multiple nodes with the same smallest even value, choose the one with the smallest index.
5. Return the smallest even value and its index in a list format.
6. If there are no even values or the array is empty, return an empty list.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def pluck(arr):
    smallest_even = float('inf')
    smallest_even_index = -1
    
    for i, num in enumerate(arr):
        if num % 2 == 0 and num < smallest_even:
            smallest_even = num
            smallest_even_index = i
    
    if smallest_even_index == -1:
        return []
    
    return [smallest_even, smallest_even_index]
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize a dictionary to store the frequency of each integer in the input list.
2. Iterate through the input list and count the frequency of each integer by updating the dictionary.
3. Iterate over the dictionary items and find the greatest integer that satisfies the condition of having a frequency greater than or equal to the value of the integer itself.
4. If such an integer is found, return it. If no such integer is found, return -1.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def search(lst):
    frequency_map = {}
    
    # Count the frequency of each integer in the input list
    for num in lst:
        if num in frequency_map:
            frequency_map[num] += 1
        else:
            frequency_map[num] = 1
    
    # Find the greatest integer that satisfies the condition
    greatest_integer = -1
    for num, freq in frequency_map.items():
        if num > greatest_integer and freq >= num:
            greatest_integer = num
    
    return greatest_integer
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. First, we need to sort the given list of integers in ascending order.
2. Then, we can create a new list where we iterate through the sorted list and alternatively append the minimum and maximum values from the sorted list to the new list.
3. Finally, return the new list as the output.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def strange_sort_list(lst):
    if not lst:
        return []
    
    sorted_lst = sorted(lst)
    result = []
    
    min_idx, max_idx = 0, len(sorted_lst) - 1
    
    while min_idx <= max_idx:
        result.append(sorted_lst[min_idx])
        if min_idx != max_idx:
            result.append(sorted_lst[max_idx])
        min_idx += 1
        max_idx -= 1
    
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the given sides can form a valid triangle by verifying the condition that the sum of any two sides should be greater than the third side.
2. If the sides form a valid triangle, calculate the area of the triangle using Heron's formula.
3. Round the calculated area to 2 decimal points.
4. If the sides do not form a valid triangle, return -1.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
import math

def triangle_area(a, b, c):
    if a + b > c and a + c > b and b + c > a:
        s = (a + b + c) / 2
        area = math.sqrt(s * (s - a) * (s - b) * (s - c))
        return round(area, 2)
    else:
        return -1
```

2. Calculate the sum of all elements in the object `q`.
3. Check if the sum of elements in the object `q` is less than or equal to the maximum possible weight `w`.
4. Return True if the object `q` is balanced and the sum of its elements is less than or equal to `w`, otherwise return False.

### Requirements

### Code
```python
def will_it_fly(q, w):
    # Check if the object q is balanced
    if str(q) == str(q)[::-1]:
        # Calculate the sum of elements in q
        total_weight = sum(q)
        # Check if the sum of elements is less than or equal to the maximum possible weight w
        if total_weight <= w:
            return True
    return False
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To find the minimum number of elements that need to be changed to make the array palindromic, we can follow these steps:
2. Initialize a variable `count` to keep track of the number of changes needed.
3. Loop through the array from both ends simultaneously.
4. For each pair of elements at symmetric positions, if they are not equal, increment the `count` by 1.
5. Repeat this process until the two pointers meet or cross each other.
6. The final `count` will be the minimum number of changes needed to make the array palindromic.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def smallest_change(arr):
    count = 0
    left, right = 0, len(arr) - 1
    
    while left < right:
        if arr[left] != arr[right]:
            count += 1
        left += 1
        right -= 1
    
    return count

# Test cases
print(smallest_change([1,2,3,5,4,7,9,6]))  # Output: 4
print(smallest_change([1,2,3,4,3,2,2]))    # Output: 1
print(smallest_change([1,2,3,2,1]))         # Output: 0
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to compare the total number of characters in all strings of two given lists.
2. If the total number of characters in the first list is less than the total number of characters in the second list, return the first list.
3. If the total number of characters in the second list is less than the total number of characters in the first list, return the second list.
4. If the total number of characters in both lists are equal, return the first list.
5. To calculate the total number of characters in a list, we sum the lengths of all strings in that list.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def total_match(lst1, lst2):
    total_chars_lst1 = sum(len(s) for s in lst1)
    total_chars_lst2 = sum(len(s) for s in lst2)
    
    if total_chars_lst1 < total_chars_lst2:
        return lst1
    elif total_chars_lst1 > total_chars_lst2:
        return lst2
    else:
        return lst1
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to check if the given number `a` is the multiplication of 3 prime numbers.
2. We can start by writing a helper function to check if a number is prime.
3. Then, we can iterate through all possible combinations of 3 prime numbers that multiply to `a`.
4. If we find such a combination, we return True. Otherwise, we return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_prime(num):
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(num ** 0.5) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def is_multiply_prime(a):
    for i in range(2, a):
        if is_prime(i):
            for j in range(i, a):
                if is_prime(j):
                    for k in range(j, a):
                        if is_prime(k) and i * j * k == a:
                            return True
    return False
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To determine if a number `x` is a simple power of another number `n`, we need to check if `n` raised to an integer power results in `x`.
2. We can start by iterating over potential integer powers of `n` starting from 1, until the result of `n**power` is greater than or equal to `x`.
3. If at any point `n**power` equals `x`, then `x` is a simple power of `n` and we return `True`. If we exhaust all powers without finding a match, we return `False`.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_simple_power(x, n):
    power = 1
    while n**power < x:
        power += 1
    return n**power == x
```

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_simple_power(x, n):
    if x == 1:
        return n == 1
    
    while x != 1:
        if x % n != 0:
            return False
        x = x // n
    
    return True
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Define a function `is_prime` to check if a number is prime by iterating from 2 to the square root of the number and checking if the number is divisible by any number in that range.
2. Define a function `hex_key` that takes a hexadecimal number as input.
3. Create a variable `prime_count` to keep track of the count of prime hexadecimal digits.
4. Iterate through each character in the input hexadecimal number.
5. For each character:
    - Convert it to a decimal integer.
    - Check if the decimal integer is a prime number.
    - If it is a prime number and it is one of the specified digits (2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13), increment the `prime_count`.
6. Return the `prime_count`.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
import math

def is_prime(n):
    if n <= 1:
        return False
    if n <= 3:
        return True
    if n % 2 == 0 or n % 3 == 0:
        return False
    i = 5
    while i * i <= n:
        if n % i == 0 or n % (i + 2) == 0:
            return False
        i += 6
    return True

def hex_key(num):
    prime_digits = set([2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13])
    prime_count = 0
    for char in num:
        decimal_value = int(char, 16)
        if is_prime(decimal_value) and decimal_value in prime_digits:
            prime_count += 1
    return prime_count
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To convert a decimal number to binary, we can use the built-in `bin()` function in Python.
2. The `bin()` function returns a string representing the binary form of the given decimal number. It includes the prefix '0b' to indicate that the result is in binary format.
3. We need to remove the '0b' prefix and add the 'db' characters at the beginning and end of the binary string before returning it.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def decimal_to_binary(decimal):
    binary_str = bin(decimal)[2:]  # Get the binary representation without the '0b' prefix
    return 'db' + binary_str + 'db'
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the length of the string is less than 3, return `False` as per the condition.
2. Iterate through the string with a step size of 3 to access every 3 consecutive letters.
3. Check if all 3 consecutive letters are distinct using a set to store unique characters. If any character is repeated, return `False`.
4. If the loop completes without returning `False`, return `True`.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_happy(s):
    if len(s) < 3:
        return False
    
    for i in range(0, len(s)-2, 3):
        if len(set(s[i:i+3])) != 3:
            return False
    
    return True
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Calculate the length of the input string.
2. Check if the length is a prime number.
3. Return True if the length is a prime number, otherwise return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_prime(n):
    if n <= 1:
        return False
    if n <= 3:
        return True
    if n % 2 == 0 or n % 3 == 0:
        return False
    i = 5
    while i * i <= n:
        if n % i == 0 or n % (i + 2) == 0:
            return False
        i += 6
    return True

def prime_length(string):
    length = len(string)
    return is_prime(length)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize a variable `count` to keep track of the count of n-digit positive integers that start or end with 1.
2. Iterate through all n-digit positive integers.
3. For each integer, check if it starts with 1 or ends with 1.
4. If it starts or ends with 1, increment the `count`.
5. Return the final `count` as the result.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def starts_one_ends(n):
    count = 0
    for i in range(10**(n-1), 10**n):
        if str(i)[0] == '1' or str(i)[-1] == '1':
            count += 1
    return count
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Convert the given positive integer `N` into its binary representation.
2. Calculate the sum of digits in the binary representation.
3. Return the sum of digits as a binary string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def solve(N):
    # Convert the integer N to binary and remove the '0b' prefix
    binary = bin(N)[2:]
    
    # Calculate the sum of digits in the binary representation
    sum_of_digits = sum(int(digit) for digit in binary)
    
    # Return the sum of digits as a binary string
    return bin(sum_of_digits)[2:]
```

def solve(N):
    # Convert the integer N to binary and remove the '0b' prefix
    binary = bin(N)[2:]
    
    # Calculate the sum of digits in the binary representation
    sum_of_digits = sum(int(digit) for digit in binary)
    
    # Return the sum of digits as a binary string
    return bin(sum_of_digits)[2:]

2. Iterate over the list `lst` starting from index 1 (odd indices).
3. Check if the element at the current index is even.
4. If the element is even, add it to `total`.
5. Return the value of `total`.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def add(lst):
    total = 0
    for i in range(1, len(lst), 2):
        if lst[i] % 2 == 0:
            total += lst[i]
    return total
```

1. Split the input string into words based on space.
2. For each word, sort the characters in ascending order based on ASCII value.
3. Join the sorted words back together with space to form the final ordered string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def anti_shuffle(s):
    words = s.split()
    ordered_words = []
    for word in words:
        ordered_chars = ''.join(sorted(word))
        ordered_words.append(ordered_chars)
    return ' '.join(ordered_words)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Iterate through each row in the 2-dimensional list.
2. For each row, check if the integer `x` is present in that row.
3. If `x` is present in the row, store the coordinates (row index, column index of `x`) in a list of tuples.
4. Sort the list of tuples by rows in ascending order.
5. Sort the coordinates of each row by columns in descending order.
6. Return the sorted list of tuples.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def get_row(lst, x):
    coordinates = []
    
    for i, row in enumerate(lst):
        for j, val in enumerate(row):
            if val == x:
                coordinates.append((i, j))
    
    coordinates.sort(key=lambda x: (x[0], -x[1]))
    
    return coordinates
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the length of the given array is 0, if so return an empty array.
2. Calculate the sum of the first and last index values of the array.
3. Check if the sum is odd or even.
4. If the sum is odd, create a copy of the array and sort it in ascending order.
5. If the sum is even, create a copy of the array and sort it in descending order.
6. Return the sorted array.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def sort_array(array):
    if len(array) == 0:
        return []
    
    sum_first_last = array[0] + array[-1]
    
    if sum_first_last % 2 == 1:
        return sorted(array)
    else:
        return sorted(array, reverse=True)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Create a dictionary to map the original alphabet to the rotated alphabet where each letter is shifted down by two multiplied by two places.
2. Iterate through each character in the input string and check if it is an alphabet character.
3. If the character is an alphabet character, find its corresponding rotated character from the dictionary and append it to the encrypted string.
4. If the character is not an alphabet character, simply append it as it is to the encrypted string.
5. Return the encrypted string as the output.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def encrypt(s):
    alphabet = 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz'
    rotated_alphabet = alphabet[4:] + alphabet[:4]  # Rotate alphabet down by 2 * 2 places
    mapping = dict(zip(alphabet, rotated_alphabet))
    
    encrypted_string = ''
    for char in s:
        if char.isalpha():
            if char.isupper():
                encrypted_string += mapping[char.lower()].upper()
            else:
                encrypted_string += mapping[char]
        else:
            encrypted_string += char

    return encrypted_string
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Split the input string `S` into sentences using the delimiters '.', '?' or '!'.
2. For each sentence, check if it starts with the word "I".
3. If a sentence starts with "I", increment the count of boredoms.
4. Return the count of boredoms.

### Code
```python
def is_bored(S):
    sentences = [sentence.strip() for sentence in re.split(r'[.!?]', S) if sentence.strip()]
    boredoms = sum(1 for sentence in sentences if sentence.startswith("I"))
    return boredoms
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if all the inputs are integers.
2. If all inputs are integers, check if any one of the numbers is equal to the sum of the other two.
3. Return True if the condition in step 2 is satisfied, otherwise return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def any_int(x, y, z):
    if all(isinstance(num, int) for num in [x, y, z]):
        if x == y + z or y == x + z or z == x + y:
            return True
    return False
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Create a dictionary to map vowels to the letter that appears 2 places ahead of them in the English alphabet.
2. Iterate through each character in the message.
3. If the character is a letter, swap its case.
4. If the character is a vowel, replace it with the corresponding value from the dictionary.
5. Return the modified message.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def encode(message):
    vowels = {'a': 'c', 'e': 'g', 'i': 'k', 'o': 'q', 'u': 'w', 'A': 'C', 'E': 'G', 'I': 'K', 'O': 'Q', 'U': 'W'}
    encoded_message = ''
    for char in message:

### Required Libraries
import string

### Code
def encode(message):
    """
    Encodes a message by swapping case of all letters and replacing vowels with the letter 2 places ahead in the alphabet.
    
    Args:
    message (str): The input message to be encoded.
    
    Returns:
    str: The encoded message.
    """
    vowels = 'aeiouAEIOU'
    encoded_message = ''
    alphabet = string.ascii_lowercase
    
    for char in message:
        if char.isalpha():
            if char.lower() in vowels:
                index = (alphabet.index(char.lower()) + 2) % 26
                encoded_message += alphabet[index].swapcase()
            else:
                encoded_message += char.swapcase()
        else:
            encoded_message += char
    
    return encoded_message

def check(candidate_func):
    """
    Checks the candidate function with a test case.
    
    Args:
    candidate_func (function): The function to be tested.
    
    Returns:
    None
    """
    assert candidate_func('Mudasir') == 'mWDCSKR', "Test case failed!"

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Define a helper function `is_prime(num)` to check if a number is prime or not.
2. Iterate through the given list of integers.
3. For each integer, check if it is prime using the `is_prime` helper function.
4. If the integer is prime, find the sum of its digits and keep track of the largest prime value found so far.
5. Finally, return the sum of digits of the largest prime value.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_prime(num):
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(num ** 0.5) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def skjkasdkd(lst):
    def digit_sum(num):
        return sum(int(digit) for digit in str(num))

    largest_prime_sum = 0
    for num in lst:
        if is_prime(num):
            current_sum = digit_sum(num)
            if current_sum > largest_prime_sum:
                largest_prime_sum = current_sum
    
    return largest_prime_sum
```

import math

def is_prime(num):
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(math.sqrt(num)) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def skjkasdkd(lst):
    def digit_sum(num):
        return sum(int(digit) for digit in str(num))

    largest_prime_sum = 0
    for num in lst:
        if is_prime(num):
            current_sum = digit_sum(num)
            if current_sum > largest_prime_sum:
                largest_prime_sum = current_sum
    
    return largest_prime_sum
```

### Code
```python
def check_dict_case(dict):
    if not dict:
        return False
    
    keys = dict.keys()
    
    if all(key.islower() for key in keys) or all(key.isupper() for key in keys):
        return True
    else:
        return False
```

### Code
```python
def check_dict_case(dict):
    if not dict:
        return False
    
    keys = [key for key in dict.keys() if isinstance(key, str)]
    
    if all(key.islower() for key in keys) or all(key.isupper() for key in keys):
        return True
    else:
        return False
```

def check_dict_case(dictionary):
    if not dictionary:
        return False
    
    keys = [key for key in dictionary.keys() if isinstance(key, str)]
    
    unique_keys = set(keys)  # Get unique keys
    
    if all(key.islower() for key in unique_keys) or all(key.isupper() for key in unique_keys):
        return True
    else:
        return False
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Define a helper function `is_prime(num)` that takes a number as input and returns True if the number is prime and False otherwise.
2. Initialize an empty list `primes` to store prime numbers less than `n`.
3. Iterate from 2 to n-1 and check if each number is prime using the `is_prime` function. If it is prime, append it to the `primes` list.
4. Return the `primes` list.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_prime(num):
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(num**0.5) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def count_up_to(n):
    primes = []
    for num in range(2, n):
        if is_prime(num):
            primes.append(num)
    return primes
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Create a variable to store the count of uppercase vowels at even indices and initialize it to 0.
2. Iterate over the characters in the string at even indices.
3. Check if the character is an uppercase vowel ('A', 'E', 'I', 'O', 'U').
4. If the character is an uppercase vowel, increment the count.
5. Return the count of uppercase vowels at even indices.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def count_upper(s):
    count = 0
    for i in range(0, len(s), 2):
        if s[i] in ['A', 'E', 'I', 'O', 'U']:
            count += 1
    return count
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Convert the input `value` which is a string representing a number to a float.
2. Calculate the floor and ceiling values of the float number.
3. Check if the difference between the input value and the floor value is less than the difference between the input value and the ceiling value.
4. If the above condition is true, return the floor value. Otherwise, return the ceiling value.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def closest_integer(value):
    num = float(value)
    floor_val = int(num)
    ceil_val = floor_val + 1
    
    if abs(num - floor_val) <= abs(num - ceil_val):
        return floor_val
    else:
        return ceil_val
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize an empty list to store the number of stones in each level.
2. Append the initial number of stones (n) to the list.
3. For each subsequent level:
    - If the current number of stones is odd, the next level will have the next odd number of stones.
    - If the current number of stones is even, the next level will have the next even number of stones.
4. Append the calculated number of stones for each level to the list.
5. Repeat step 3 until reaching n levels.
6. Return the list containing the number of stones in each level.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def make_a_pile(n):
    stones_per_level = [n]  # Initialize the list with the first level stones
    for i in range(n - 1):  # Loop through n-1 levels
        if stones_per_level[-1] % 2 == 0:  # Check if the last level stones are even
            stones_per_level.append(stones_per_level[-1] + 1)  # Next level with next odd number of stones
        else:
            stones_per_level.append(stones_per_level[-1] + 2)  # Next level with next even number of stones
    return stones_per_level
```

def words_string(s):

def choose_num(x, y):
    max_even = -1
    for num in range(x, y+1):
        if num % 2 == 0 and num > max_even:
            max_even = num
    return max_even

def rounded_avg(n, m):
    if n > m:
        return -1
    avg = round((n + m) / 2)
    return bin(avg)

```python
def unique_digits(x):
    def has_even_digit(num):
        for digit in str(num):
            if int(digit) % 2 == 0:
                return True
        return False

    result = [num for num in x if not has_even_digit(num)]
    return sorted(result)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Sort the array to have integers between 1 and 9 inclusive.
2. Reverse the sorted array.
3. Replace each digit by its corresponding name from "One", "Two", "Three", "Four", "Five", "Six", "Seven", "Eight", "Nine".
4. If the array is empty, return an empty array.
5. If the array has any strange number, ignore it.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def by_length(arr):
    if not arr:  # Check if the array is empty
        return []
    
    # Filter out integers between 1 and 9 inclusive
    filtered_arr = [x for x in arr if 1 <= x <= 9]
    
    # Sort the filtered array
    sorted_arr = sorted(filtered_arr)
    
    # Reverse the sorted array
    reversed_arr = sorted_arr[::-1]
    
    # Map each digit to its corresponding name
    digits_map = {
        1: "One",
        2: "Two",
        3: "Three",
        4: "Four",
        5: "Five",
        6: "Six",
        7: "Seven",
        8: "Eight",
        9: "Nine"
    }
    
    result = [digits_map[digit] for digit in reversed_arr]  # Replace digits with names
    
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Create an empty list to store the output.
2. Iterate over the range from 1 to n (inclusive).
3. For each index i:
   - If i is even, calculate the factorial of i.
   - If i is odd, calculate the sum of numbers from 1 to i.
4. Append the result to the output list.
5. Return the output list.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def f(n):
    def factorial(num):
        if num == 0:
            return 1
        else:
            return num * factorial(num - 1)
        
    result = []
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        if i % 2 == 0:
            result.append(factorial(i))
        else:
            result.append(sum(range(1, i+1)))
    
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Define a function that takes a positive integer `n` as input.
2. Initialize two variables `even_count` and `odd_count` to keep track of the count of even and odd palindromes, both set to 0 initially.
3. Iterate through the range from 1 to n (inclusive).
4. For each number in the range, check if it is a palindrome.
5. If the number is a palindrome, increment the corresponding count based on whether it is even or odd.
6. Finally, return a tuple containing the counts of even and odd palindromes.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_palindrome(num):
    return str(num) == str(num)[::-1]

def even_odd_palindrome(n):
    even_count = 0
    odd_count = 0

    for i in range(1, n+1):
        if is_palindrome(i):
            if i % 2 == 0:
                even_count += 1
            else:
                odd_count += 1

    return (even_count, odd_count)
```

2. Iterate through each number in the input array.
3. For each number, calculate the sum of its digits. If the number is negative, consider its signed digits.
4. If the sum of digits is greater than 0, increment the `count`.
5. Finally, return the `count`.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def count_nums(arr):
    count = 0
    for num in arr:
        if num < 0:
            num_str = str(num)[1:]
        else:
            num_str = str(num)
        
        digit_sum = sum(int(d) for d in num_str)
        if digit_sum > 0:
            count += 1
    
    return count
```

from typing import List

def count_digits(num: int) -> int:
    if num < 0:
        num_str = str(num)[1:]
    else:
        num_str = str(num)
        
    digit_sum = sum(int(d) for d in num_str)
    return digit_sum

def count_nums(arr: List[int]) -> int:
    count = 0
    for num in arr:
        if count_digits(num) > 0:
            count += 1
    
    return count
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the given array is empty. If it is empty, return True as the array is already sorted.
2. Find the index of the minimum element in the array.
3. Check if the array is already sorted in non-decreasing order. If it is sorted, return True.
4. Determine the number of right shifts required to move the minimum element to the beginning of the array.
5. Create a new array by slicing the input array from the index of the minimum element to the end, and then from the beginning to the index of the minimum element.
6. Check if the new array is sorted in non-decreasing order. If it is sorted, return True; otherwise, return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def move_one_ball(arr):
    if not arr:
        return True
    
    min_idx = arr.index(min(arr))
    if arr == sorted(arr):
        return True
    
    shifts = len(arr) - min_idx
    new_arr = arr[min_idx:] + arr[:min_idx]
    
    return new_arr == sorted(new_arr)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to check if it is possible to exchange elements between `lst1` and `lst2` to make all elements in `lst1` even.
2. We will count the number of even elements in `lst1` and the total number of elements in `lst1`.
3. If the count of even elements is equal to the total number of elements in `lst1`, we can return "YES" as all elements in `lst1` are already even.
4. If the count of even elements is less than the total number of elements in `lst1`, we need to check if it is possible to exchange elements from `lst2` to make all elements in `lst1` even.
5. To do this, we can iterate over `lst2` and for each element, check if it is odd and can be exchanged with an element in `lst1` to make `lst1` all even.
6. If we can find exchanges for all odd elements in `lst1`, we return "YES" indicating it is possible, otherwise, we return "NO".

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def exchange(lst1, lst2):
    count_even = sum(1 for num in lst1 if num % 2 == 0)
    total_elements = len(lst1)
    
    if count_even == total_elements:
        return "YES"
    
    for num in lst1:
        if num % 2 != 0:
            for i, num2 in enumerate(lst2):
                if num2 % 2 == 0:
                    lst1[lst1.index(num)] = num2
                    lst2[i] = num
                    break
            else:
                return "NO"
    
    return "YES"
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Split the input string by space to get individual letters.
2. Create a dictionary to store the count of each letter.
3. Iterate through the letters and update the count in the dictionary.
4. Find the maximum count value in the dictionary.
5. Create a new dictionary containing only the letters with the maximum count value.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def histogram(test):
    letters = test.split()
    letter_count = {}
    
    for letter in letters:
        if letter in letter_count:
            letter_count[letter] += 1
        else:
            letter_count[letter] = 1
            
    max_count = max(letter_count.values())
    result = {key: value for key, value in letter_count.items() if value == max_count}
    
    return result
```

```python
def histogram(test):
    letters = test.split()
    letter_count = {}
    
    for letter in letters:
        if letter in letter_count:
            letter_count[letter] += 1
        else:
            letter_count[letter] = 1
    
    if not letter_count:  # Check if the dictionary is empty
        return {}
    
    max_count = max(letter_count.values())
    result = {key: value for key, value in letter_count.items() if value == max_count}
    
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Remove all characters in string `s` that are present in string `c`.
2. Check if the resulting string is a palindrome.
3. Return a tuple containing the result string and True/False for the palindrome check.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def reverse_delete(s, c):
    # Remove characters in s that are present in c
    result = ''.join(char for char in s if char not in c)
    
    # Check if the result is a palindrome
    is_palindrome = result == result[::-1]
    
    return result, is_palindrome
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to find the minimum sum of any non-empty sub-array within the given array of integers.
2. Initialize a variable `min_sum` to a large value or `float('inf')` to keep track of the minimum sum found so far.
3. Iterate over all possible sub-arrays using two nested loops. The outer loop will determine the starting index of the sub-array, and the inner loop will determine the ending index.
4. For each sub-array, calculate the sum and update `min_sum` if the current sum is less than `min_sum`.
5. After iterating through all possible sub-arrays, return the `min_sum`.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def minSubArraySum(nums):
    min_sum = float('inf')
    for i in range(len(nums)):
        current_sum = 0
        for j in range(i, len(nums)):
            current_sum += nums[j]
            min_sum = min(min_sum, current_sum)
    return min_sum
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to iterate over each well in the grid and calculate the amount of water in each well.
2. We then need to calculate the number of times we need to lower the buckets to empty each well.
3. Finally, we sum up the number of times we need to lower the buckets for all wells to get the total number of times needed.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def max_fill(grid, capacity):
    if not grid:
        return 0
    
    total_buckets = 0
    for well in grid:
        water_in_well = sum(well)
        total_buckets += math.ceil(water_in_well / capacity)
    
    return total_buckets
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to sort the given array of non-negative integers based on the number of ones in their binary representation in ascending order.
2. If two numbers have the same number of ones in their binary representation, we need to sort them based on their decimal value.
3. We will first define a custom sorting key function that calculates the number of ones in the binary representation of a number and then the number itself.
4. We will use this custom sorting key function with the `sorted` function to sort the array.
5. We will return the sorted array.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def sort_array(arr):
    def custom_sort_key(num):
        return bin(num).count('1'), num

    return sorted(arr, key=custom_sort_key)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Split the input string `s` into individual words.
2. For each word, count the number of consonants in it.
3. If the count of consonants in a word is equal to the input `n`, then add that word to the result list.
4. Return the list of words that contain exactly `n` consonants.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def select_words(s, n):
    def count_consonants(word):
        consonants = set("bcdfghjklmnpqrstvwxyzBCDFGHJKLMNPQRSTVWXYZ")
        return sum(1 for letter in word if letter in consonants)

    words = s.split()
    result = [word for word in words if count_consonants(word) == n]
    return result
```

def get_closest_vowel(word):
    vowels = set('aeiouAEIOU')
    for i in range(1, len(word) - 1):
        if word[i] not in vowels and word[i-1] in vowels and word[i+1] in vowels:
            return word[i]
    return ""

### Code
```
def get_closest_vowel(word):
    vowels = set('aeiouAEIOU')
    for i in range(1, len(word) - 1):
        if word[i] not in vowels and word[i-1] in vowels and word[i+1] in vowels:
            return word[i]
    return ""

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We are given a list of two strings containing only open and close parentheses.
2. We need to check if it is possible to concatenate the two strings in some order to form a good string with balanced parentheses.
3. To achieve this, we can simulate the scenario by tracking the count of open and close parentheses.
4. We can iterate through both strings and increment the count for open parentheses and decrement the count for close parentheses.
5. If at any point the count becomes negative or the final count is not zero, then it is not possible to form a good string.
6. If the count remains non-negative and becomes zero at the end, then it is possible to form a good string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def match_parens(lst):
    open_count = 0
    close_count = 0

    for string in lst:
        for char in string:
            if char == '(':
                open_count += 1
            elif char == ')':
                if open_count > 0:
                    open_count -= 1
                else:
                    return 'No'
    
    return 'Yes' if open_count == 0 else 'No'

# Test cases
print(match_parens(['()(', ')']))  # Output: 'Yes'
print(match_parens([')', ')']))     # Output: 'No'
```

### Requirements

### Code
```
def solution(lst):
    return sum(x for i, x in enumerate(lst) if i % 2 == 0 and x % 2 != 0)

    total_sum = 0
    
    for i in range(k):
        if abs(arr[i]) < 100:
            total_sum += arr[i]
    
    return total_sum

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize an empty list to store the odd numbers in the Collatz sequence.
2. Start with the given positive integer `n`.
3. Calculate the Collatz sequence for `n` by following the rules:
    - If the number is odd, multiply it by 3 and add 1.
    - If the number is even, divide it by 2.
    - Repeat these steps until the sequence reaches 1.
4. While calculating the sequence, keep track of the odd numbers encountered.
5. Sort the odd numbers in increasing order.
6. Return the sorted list of odd numbers in the Collatz sequence.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    odd_collatz = []
    while n != 1:
        if n % 2 == 0:
            n = n // 2
        else:
            odd_collatz.append(n)
            n = 3 * n + 1
    odd_collatz.append(1)  # Add 1 to the sequence
    return sorted(odd_collatz)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. First, we need to check if the input date string is not empty.
2. Then, we need to split the date string into day, month, and year.
3. Next, we need to check if the month is between 1 and 12.
4. Depending on the month, we need to validate the number of days:
   - For months 1, 3, 5, 7, 8, 10, and 12, the number of days should be between 1 and 31.
   - For months 4, 6, 9, and 11, the number of days should be between 1 and 30.
   - For February (month 2), the number of days should be between 1 and 29.
5. If all these conditions are satisfied, return True. Otherwise, return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def valid_date(date):
    if not date:
        return False
    
    date_parts = date.split('-')
    if len(date_parts) != 3:
        return False
    
    month = int(date_parts[0])
    day = int(date_parts[1])
    year = int(date_parts[2])
    
    if month < 1 or month > 12:
        return False
    
    if month in [1, 3, 5, 7, 8, 10, 12] and (day < 1 or day > 31):
        return False
    elif month in [4, 6, 9, 11] and (day < 1 or day > 30):
        return False
    elif month == 2:
        if (year % 4 == 0 and year % 100 != 0) or (year % 400 == 0):
            if day < 1 or day > 29:
                return False
        else:
            if day < 1 or day > 28:
                return False
    
    return True
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the input list is empty or has only one element, return True as it is considered sorted.
2. Initialize a variable `prev` to keep track of the previous element in the list.
3. Iterate through the list and compare each element with the previous element.
4. If any element is less than the previous element or if there are more than one duplicate of the same number, return False.
5. If the entire list is iterated without any issues, return True.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def is_sorted(lst):
    if len(lst) <= 1:
        return True
    
    prev = lst[0]
    duplicates = {}
    
    for num in lst[1:]:
        if num < prev or num in duplicates:
            return False
        elif num == prev:
            if num in duplicates:
                return False
            duplicates[num] = 1
        prev = num
    
    return True
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Determine the intersection of the two given intervals.
2. Calculate the length of the intersection.
3. Check if the length of the intersection is a prime number.
4. Return "YES" if the length is prime, otherwise return "NO".
5. If the two intervals don't intersect, return "NO".

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_prime(n):
    if n <= 1:
        return False
    if n <= 3:
        return True
    if n % 2 == 0 or n % 3 == 0:
        return False
    i = 5
    while i * i <= n:
        if n % i == 0 or n % (i + 2) == 0:
            return False
        i += 6
    return True

def intersection(interval1, interval2):
    start1, end1 = interval1
    start2, end2 = interval2
    
    intersection_start = max(start1, start2)
    intersection_end = min(end1, end2)
    
    if intersection_start > intersection_end:
        return "NO"
    
    intersection_length = intersection_end - intersection_start + 1
    if is_prime(intersection_length):
        return "YES"
    else:
        return "NO"
```
